When the isotope decays, it may emit [alpha particles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_particles "Alpha particles"), [protons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protons "Protons"), or [gamma rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma_rays "Gamma rays"). Alpha particles, once stabilized by capturing electrons, form helium atoms which accumulate at grain boundaries and may result in swelling, blistering, or embrittlement of the material.[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Shin_Kajita_2014-114)[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-115)

### Selection of materials

[Tungsten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tungsten "Tungsten") is widely regarded as the optimal material for plasma-facing components in next-generation fusion devices due to its unique properties and potential for enhancements. Its low sputtering rates and high melting point make it particularly suitable for the high-stress environments of fusion reactors, allowing it to withstand intense conditions without rapid degradation. Additionally, tungsten's low [tritium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tritium "Tritium") retention through co-deposition and implantation is essential in fusion contexts, as it helps to minimize the accumulation of this radioactive isotope.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-116)[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-117)[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-118)[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-119)

Liquid metals (lithium, [gallium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallium "Gallium"), [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin "Tin")) have been proposed, e.g., by injection of 1–5mm thick streams flowing at 10m/s on solid substrates.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Graphite features a gross erosion rate due to physical and chemical [sputtering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputtering "Sputtering") amounting to many meters per year, requiring redeposition of the sputtered material. The redeposition site generally does not exactly match the sputter site, allowing net erosion that may be prohibitive. An even larger problem is that tritium is redeposited with the redeposited graphite. The tritium inventory in the wall and dust could build up to many kilograms, representing a waste of resources and a radiological hazard in case of an accident. Graphite found favor as material for short-lived experiments, but appears unlikely to become the primary [plasma-facing material](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma-facing_material "Plasma-facing material") (PFM) in a commercial reactor.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Materials-105)[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-120)

Ceramic materials such as silicon carbide (SiC) have similar issues like graphite. Tritium retention in silicon carbide plasma-facing components is approximately 1.5–2 times higher than in graphite, resulting in reduced fuel efficiency and heightened safety risks in fusion reactors. SiC tends to trap more tritium, limiting its availability for fusion and increasing the risk of hazardous accumulation, complicating tritium management.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-121)[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-122) Furthermore, the chemical and physical sputtering of SiC remains significant, contributing to tritium buildup through co-deposition over time and with increasing particle fluence. As a result, carbon-based materials have been excluded from [ITER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITER "ITER"), [DEMO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DEMOnstration_Power_Plant "DEMOnstration Power Plant"), and similar devices.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-123)

Tungsten's sputtering rate is orders of magnitude smaller than carbon's, and tritium is much less incorporated into redeposited tungsten. However, tungsten plasma impurities are much more damaging than carbon impurities, and self-sputtering can be high, requiring the plasma in contact with the tungsten not be too hot (a few tens of eV rather than hundreds of eV). Tungsten also has issues around eddy currents and melting in off-normal events, as well as some radiological issues.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Materials-105)

Recent advances in materials for containment apparatus materials have found that certain ceramics can actually improve the longevity of the material of the containment apparatus. Studies on [MAX phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MAX_phases "MAX phases"), such as titanium silicon carbide, show that under the high operating temperatures of nuclear fusion, the material undergoes a phase transformation from a hexagonal structure to a face-centered-cubic (FCC) structure, driven by helium bubble growth. Helium atoms preferentially accumulate in the Si layer of the hexagonal structure, as the Si atoms are more mobile than the Ti–C slabs. As more atoms are trapped, the Ti–C slab is peeled off, causing the Si atoms to become highly mobile interstitial atoms in the new FCC structure. Lattice strain induced by the He bubbles cause Si atoms to diffuse out of compressive areas, typically towards the surface of the material, forming a protective silicon dioxide layer.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-124)

Doping vessel materials with iron silicate has emerged as a promising approach to enhance containment materials in fusion reactors, as well. This method targets helium embrittlement at grain boundaries, a common issue that arises as helium atoms accumulate and form bubbles. Over time, these bubbles coalesce at grain boundaries, causing them to expand and degrade the material's structural integrity. By contrast, introducing iron silicate creates [nucleation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleation "Nucleation") sites within the metal matrix that are more thermodynamically favorable for helium aggregation. This localized congregation around iron silicate nanoparticles induces matrix strain rather than weakening grain boundaries, preserving the material's strength and longevity.[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-125)[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-126)

## Accident scenarios and the environment

### Accident potential

Accident potential and effect on the environment are critical to social acceptance of nuclear fusion, also known as a [social license](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_social_responsibility#Social_license_to_operate "Corporate social responsibility").[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-127) Fusion reactors are not subject to [catastrophic meltdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_meltdown "Nuclear meltdown").[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-McCrackenStott2012-128) It requires precise and controlled temperature, pressure and magnetic field parameters to produce net energy, and any damage or loss of required control would rapidly quench the reaction.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-afraid-129) Fusion reactors operate with seconds or even microseconds worth of fuel at any moment. Without active refueling, the reactions immediately quench.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-McCrackenStott2012-128)

The same constraints prevent runaway reactions. Although the plasma is expected to have a volume of 1,000m3 (35,000ft3) or more, the plasma typically contains only a few grams of fuel.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-McCrackenStott2012-128) By comparison, a fission reactor is typically loaded with enough fuel for months or years, and no additional fuel is necessary to continue the reaction. This large fuel supply is what offers the possibility of a meltdown.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-n-130)

In magnetic containment, strong fields develop in coils that are mechanically held in place by the reactor structure. Failure of this structure could release this tension and allow the magnet to "explode" outward. The severity of this event would be similar to other industrial accidents or an [MRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MRI "MRI") machine quench/explosion, and could be effectively contained within a [containment building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment_building "Containment building") similar to those used in fission reactors.

In laser-driven inertial containment, the larger size of the reaction chamber reduces the stress on materials. Although failure of the reaction chamber is possible, stopping fuel delivery prevents catastrophic failure.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-economic_prospects-131)

### Magnet quench

A [magnet quench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnet_quench "Magnet quench") is an abnormal termination of magnet operation that occurs when part of the superconducting coil exits the superconducting state (becomes normal). This can occur because the field inside the magnet is too large, the rate of change of field is too large (causing [eddy currents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddy_current "Eddy current") and resultant [heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joule_heating "Joule heating") in the copper support matrix), or a combination of the two.

More rarely a magnet defect can cause a quench. When this happens, that particular spot is subject to rapid [Joule heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joule_heating "Joule heating") from the current, which raises the [temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature "Temperature") of the surrounding regions. This pushes those regions into the normal state as well, which leads to more heating in a chain reaction. The entire magnet rapidly becomes normal over several seconds, depending on the size of the superconducting coil. This is accompanied by a loud bang as the energy in the magnetic field is converted to heat, and the [cryogenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryogenic "Cryogenic") fluid boils away. The sudden decrease of current can result in [kilovolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilovolt "Kilovolt") inductive voltage spikes and arcing. Permanent damage to the magnet is not likely to happen, but components can be damaged by localized heating, high voltages, or large mechanical forces.

In practice, magnets usually have safety devices to stop or limit the current when a quench is detected. If a large magnet undergoes a quench, the inert vapor formed by the evaporating cryogenic fluid can present a significant [asphyxiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asphyxiation "Asphyxiation") hazard to operators by displacing breathable air.

A large section of the superconducting magnets in [CERN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CERN "CERN")'s [Large Hadron Collider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Hadron_Collider "Large Hadron Collider") unexpectedly quenched during start-up operations in 2008, destroying multiple magnets.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-132) In order to prevent a recurrence, the LHC's superconducting magnets are equipped with fast-ramping heaters that are activated when a quench event is detected. The dipole bending magnets are connected in series. Each power circuit includes 154 individual magnets, and should a quench event occur, the entire combined stored energy of these magnets must be dumped at once. This energy is transferred into massive blocks of metal that heat up to several hundred degrees Celsius—because of resistive heating—in seconds. A magnet quench is a "fairly routine event" during the operation of a particle accelerator.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-133)

### Atmospheric tritium release

The natural product of the fusion reaction is a small amount of [helium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helium "Helium"), which is harmless to life. Hazardous tritium is difficult to retain completely.

Although tritium is volatile and biologically active, the health risk posed by a release is much lower than that of most radioactive contaminants, because of tritium's short half-life (12.32 years) and very low decay energy (~14.95keV), and because it does not [bioaccumulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioaccumulate "Bioaccumulate") (it cycles out of the body as water, with a [biological half-life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_half-life "Biological half-life") of 7 to 14 days).[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-nuclearsafety-petrangeli-134) ITER incorporates total containment facilities for tritium.[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-ITER-135)

Calculations suggest that about 1 kilogram (2.2lb) of tritium and other radioactive gases in a typical power station would be present. The amount is small enough that it would dilute to legally acceptable limits by the time they reached the station's [perimeter fence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perimeter_fence "Perimeter fence").[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-WorldEnergyCouncil-136)

The likelihood of small industrial accidents, including the local release of radioactivity and injury to staff, are estimated to be minor compared to fission. They would include accidental releases of lithium or tritium or mishandling of radioactive reactor components.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-economic_prospects-131)

### Radioactive waste

See also: [Radioactive waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_waste "Radioactive waste")

Fusion reactors create far less radioactive material than fission reactors. Furthermore, the material it creates is less damaging biologically, and the radioactivity dissipates within a time period that is well within existing engineering capabilities for safe long-term waste storage.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-demonstration-137) In specific terms, except in the case of [aneutronic fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aneutronic_fusion "Aneutronic fusion"),[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-138)[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Diffusion-139) the neutron flux turns the structural materials radioactive. The amount of radioactive material at shutdown may be comparable to that of a fission reactor, with important differences. The half-lives of fusion and neutron activation [radioisotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioisotopes "Radioisotopes") tend to be less than those from fission, so that the hazard decreases more rapidly. Whereas fission reactors produce waste that remains radioactive for thousands of years, the radioactive material in a fusion reactor (other than tritium) would be the reactor core itself, and most of this would be radioactive for about 50 years, with other low-level waste being radioactive for another 100 years or so thereafter.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-140) The fusion waste's short half-life eliminates the challenge of long-term storage. After 500 years, the material's levels of [radiotoxicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionizing_radiation "Ionizing radiation") would be the same as [coal ash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_ash "Coal ash").[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-WorldEnergyCouncil-136)
Nonetheless, classification as intermediate level waste rather than low-level waste may complicate safety discussions.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141)[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-demonstration-137)

The choice of materials is less constrained than in conventional fission, where many materials are required for their specific neutron cross sections. Fusion reactors can be designed using "low activation", materials that do not easily become radioactive. [Vanadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanadium "Vanadium"), for example, becomes much less radioactive than [stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stainless_steel "Stainless steel").[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-142) [Carbon fiber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_fiber "Carbon fiber") materials are also low-activation, are strong and light, and are promising for laser-inertial reactors where a magnetic field is not required.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-143)

### Fuel reserves

Fusion power commonly proposes the use of deuterium as fuel, and many current designs also use [lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium"). Assuming a fusion energy output equal to the 1995 global power output of about 100 [E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exa- "Exa-") J/yr (= 1 × 1020 J/yr) and that this does not increase in the future, which is unlikely, then known current lithium reserves would last 3000 years. On the other hand, lithium from seawater would last 60 million years, and, together with utilizing a more complicated fusion process using only deuterium, would provide fuel for 150 billion years.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Ongena-144)

## Potential military usage

Main article: [Nuclear proliferation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_proliferation "Nuclear proliferation")

In some scenarios, fusion power technology could be adapted to produce materials for military purposes. A fusion power station could produce a significant amount of [tritium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tritium "Tritium"); tritium is used in the trigger of hydrogen bombs and in modern [boosted fission weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boosted_fission_weapon "Boosted fission weapon"), but it can be produced in other ways. The energetic neutrons from a fusion reactor could be used to breed weapons-grade [plutonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutonium "Plutonium") or [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") for an atomic bomb (for example, by transmutation of 238

U to 239

Pu, or 232

Th to 233

U).

A study conducted in 2011 assessed three scenarios:[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-ProliferationRisk_Goldston-145)

- Small-scale fusion station: As a result of much higher power consumption, heat dissipation, and a more recognizable design compared to enrichment [gas centrifuges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_centrifuge "Gas centrifuge"), this choice would be much easier to detect and therefore implausible.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-ProliferationRisk_Goldston-145)
- Commercial facility: The production potential is significant. But no fertile or fissile substances necessary for the production of weapon-usable materials need to be present at a civil fusion system at all. If not shielded, detection of these materials can be done by their characteristic [gamma radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma_radiation "Gamma radiation"). The underlying redesign could be detected by regular design information verification. In the (technically more feasible) case of solid breeder blanket modules, it would be necessary for incoming components to be inspected for the presence of fertile material,[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-ProliferationRisk_Goldston-145) otherwise plutonium for several weapons could be produced each year.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-StrongNeutronSources-146)
- Prioritizing weapon-grade material regardless of secrecy: The fastest way to produce weapon-usable material was seen in modifying a civil fusion power station. No weapons-compatible material is required during civil use. Even without the need for covert action, such a modification would take about two months to start production and at least an additional week to generate a significant amount. This was considered to be enough time to detect a military use and to react with diplomatic or military means. To stop the production, a military destruction of parts of the facility while leaving out the reactor would be sufficient.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-ProliferationRisk_Goldston-145)

Another study concluded "...large fusion reactors—even if not designed for fissile material breeding—could easily produce several hundred kg Pu per year with high weapon quality and very low source material requirements." It was emphasized that the implementation of features for intrinsic proliferation resistance might only be possible at an early phase of research and development.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-StrongNeutronSources-146) The theoretical and computational tools needed for hydrogen bomb design are closely related to those needed for [inertial confinement fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertial_confinement_fusion "Inertial confinement fusion"), but have very little in common with magnetic confinement fusion.

| Feedstock | Product | Usage |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Lithium-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium-6 "Lithium-6") | [Tritium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tritium "Tritium") | [Boosted fission weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boosted_fission_weapon "Boosted fission weapon"), [fusion weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_weapon "Fusion weapon") |
| [Thorium-232](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thorium-232 "Thorium-232") | [Uranium-233](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium-233 "Uranium-233") | [Fission weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fission_weapon "Fission weapon") |
| [Uranium-235](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium-235 "Uranium-235") |
| [Uranium-238](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium-238 "Uranium-238") | [Plutonium-239](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutonium-239 "Plutonium-239") |
| [Neptunium-237](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neptunium-237 "Neptunium-237") | Hypothetical fission weapons |
| [Americium-241](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americium-241 "Americium-241") | [Americium-242m](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americium-242m "Americium-242m") |
| [Curium-244](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curium-244 "Curium-244") | [Curium-245](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curium-245 "Curium-245") |

## Economics

### Historic investment

The [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union") spent almost €10 billion through the 1990s.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-148) [ITER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITER "ITER") represents an investment of over twenty billion dollars, and possibly tens of billions more, including [in kind contributions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_kind "In kind").[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-149)[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-150) Under the European Union's [Sixth Framework Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Framework_Programme "Sixth Framework Programme"), nuclear fusion research received €750 million (in addition to ITER funding), compared with €810 million for sustainable energy research,[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-151) putting research into fusion power well ahead of that of any single rival technology. The [United States Department of Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Energy "United States Department of Energy") has allocated $US367M–$US671M every year since 2010, peaking in 2020,[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-152) with plans to reduce investment to $US425M in its FY2021 Budget Request.[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-153) About a quarter of this budget is directed to support ITER.

The size of the investments and time lines meant that fusion research was traditionally almost exclusively publicly funded. However, starting in the 2010s, the promise of commercializing a paradigm-changing [low-carbon energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-carbon_energy "Low-carbon energy") source began to attract a raft of companies and investors.[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-154) Over two dozen start-up companies attracted over one billion dollars from roughly 2000 to 2020, mainly from 2015, and a further three billion in funding and milestone related commitments in 2021,[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-155)[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-156) with investors including [Jeff Bezos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Bezos "Jeff Bezos"), [Peter Thiel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Thiel "Peter Thiel"), and [Bill Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Gates "Bill Gates"), as well as institutional investors including [Legal & General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_&_General "Legal & General"), and energy companies including [Equinor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equinor "Equinor"), [Eni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eni "Eni"), [Chevron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_Corporation "Chevron Corporation"),[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Space_Race-157) and the Chinese [ENN Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ENN_Group "ENN Group").[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Pearson-2020-69)[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-158)[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Diffusion-139) In 2021, [Commonwealth Fusion Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Fusion_Systems "Commonwealth Fusion Systems") (CFS) obtained $1.8 billion in scale-up funding, and [Helion Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helion_Energy "Helion Energy") obtained a half-billion dollars with an additional $1.7 billion contingent on meeting milestones.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Sets_Sights-159)

Scenarios developed in the 2000s and early 2010s discussed the effects of the commercialization of fusion power on the future of human civilization.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Giant_Step-160) Using nuclear fission as a guide, these saw ITER and later [DEMO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DEMOnstration_Power_Station "DEMOnstration Power Station") as bringing online the first commercial reactors around 2050 and a rapid expansion after mid-century.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Giant_Step-160) Some scenarios emphasized "fusion nuclear science facilities" as a step beyond ITER.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-161)[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-pilot_plants-162) However, the economic obstacles to tokamak-based fusion power remain immense, requiring investment to fund prototype tokamak reactors[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-163) and development of new supply chains,[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-164) a problem which will affect any kind of fusion reactor.[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-165) Tokamak designs appear to be labour-intensive,[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-166) while the commercialization risk of alternatives like inertial fusion energy is high due to the lack of government resources.[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-167)

Scenarios since 2010 note computing and material science advances enabling multi-phase national or cost-sharing "Fusion Pilot Plants" (FPPs) along various technology pathways,[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-168)[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-pilot_plants-162)[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Tokamak-169)[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-170)[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-171)[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-172) such as the UK [Spherical Tokamak for Energy Production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spherical_Tokamak_for_Energy_Production "Spherical Tokamak for Energy Production"), within the 2030–2040 time frame.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-173)[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-174)[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Early_Markets-175) Notably, in June 2021, General Fusion announced it would accept the UK government's offer to host the world's first substantial [public-private partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership "Public-private partnership") fusion demonstration plant, at [Culham Centre for Fusion Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culham_Centre_for_Fusion_Energy "Culham Centre for Fusion Energy").[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-176) The plant will be constructed from 2022 to 2025 and is intended to lead the way for commercial pilot plants in the late 2020s. The plant will be 70% of full scale and is expected to attain a stable plasma of 150 million degrees.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-177) Further UK plans include a development site near [Retford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retford "Retford").[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-178) In the United States, cost-sharing public-private partnership FPPs appear likely,[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-179) and in 2022 the DOE announced a new Milestone-Based Fusion Development Program as the centerpiece of its Bold Decadal Vision for Commercial Fusion Energy,[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-180) which envisages private sector-led teams delivering FPP pre-conceptual designs, defining technology roadmaps, and pursuing the R&D necessary to resolve critical-path scientific and technical issues towards an FPP design.[\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-181) Compact reactor technology based on such demonstration plants may enable commercialization via a fleet approach from the 2030s[\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-182) if early markets can be located.[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Early_Markets-175)

### Relative costs

The widespread adoption of non-nuclear renewable energy has transformed the energy landscape. Such renewables are projected to supply 74% of global energy by 2050.[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-183) The steady fall of renewable energy prices challenges the economic competitiveness of fusion power.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/20201019_Levelized_Cost_of_Energy_%28LCOE%2C_Lazard%29_-_renewable_energy.svg/960px-20201019_Levelized_Cost_of_Energy_%28LCOE%2C_Lazard%29_-_renewable_energy.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:20201019_Levelized_Cost_of_Energy_(LCOE,_Lazard)_-_renewable_energy.svg) Levelized cost of energy (LCOE) for various sources of energy including wind, solar and nuclear energy[\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-LazardLCOE-184)

Some economists suggest fusion power is unlikely to match other [renewable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy "Renewable energy") costs.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141) Fusion plants are expected to face large start up and [capital costs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_cost "Capital cost"). Moreover, operation and maintenance are likely to be costly.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141) While the costs of the [China Fusion Engineering Test Reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Fusion_Engineering_Test_Reactor "China Fusion Engineering Test Reactor") are not well known, an EU DEMO fusion concept was projected to feature a [levelized cost of energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levelized_cost_of_energy "Levelized cost of energy") (LCOE) of $121/MWh.[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-185)

Fuel costs are low, but economists suggest that the energy cost for a one-gigawatt plant would increase by $16.5 per MWh for every $1 billion increase in the capital investment in construction. There is also the risk that easily obtained lithium will be used up making batteries. Obtaining it from seawater would be very costly and might require more energy than the energy that would be generated.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141)

In contrast, [renewable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy "Renewable energy") levelized cost of energy estimates are substantially lower. For instance, the 2019 levelized cost of energy of [solar energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_energy "Solar energy") was estimated to be $40–46/MWh, [on shore wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onshore_wind_farm "Onshore wind farm") was estimated at $29–56/MWh, and [offshore wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_wind "Offshore wind") was approximately $92/MWh.[\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-186)

### Prospects

However, fusion power may still have a role filling energy gaps left by renewables,[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Early_Markets-175)[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141) depending on how administration priorities for energy and environmental justice influence the market.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Sets_Sights-159) In the 2020s, socioeconomic studies of fusion that began to consider these factors emerged,[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-187) and in 2022 EUROFusion launched its Socio-Economic Studies and Prospective Research and Development strands to investigate how such factors might affect commercialization pathways and timetables.[\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-188) Similarly, in April 2023 Japan announced a national strategy to industrialise fusion.[\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-189) Thus, fusion power may work in tandem with other renewable energy sources rather than becoming the primary energy source.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141) In some applications, fusion power could provide the base load, especially if including integrated thermal storage and cogeneration and considering the potential for retrofitting coal plants.[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Early_Markets-175)[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141)

## Regulation

As fusion pilot plants move within reach, legal and regulatory issues must be addressed.[\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-190) In September 2020, the United States [National Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Sciences "National Academy of Sciences") consulted with private fusion companies to consider a national pilot plant. The following month, the [United States Department of Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Energy "United States Department of Energy"), the [Nuclear Regulatory Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Regulatory_Commission "Nuclear Regulatory Commission") (NRC) and the [Fusion Industry Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_Industry_Association "Fusion Industry Association") co-hosted a public forum to begin the process.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Space_Race-157) In November 2020, the [International Atomic Energy Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Atomic_Energy_Agency "International Atomic Energy Agency") (IAEA) began working with various nations to create safety standards[\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Safety_in_Fusion-191) such as dose regulations and [radioactive waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_waste "Radioactive waste") handling.[\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Safety_in_Fusion-191) In January and March 2021, NRC hosted two public meetings on regulatory frameworks.[\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-192)[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-193) A public-private cost-sharing approach was endorsed in the December 27 H.R.133 Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2021, which authorized $325 million over five years for a partnership program to build fusion demonstration facilities, with a 100% match from private industry.[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-194)

Subsequently, the UK Regulatory Horizons Council published a report calling for a fusion regulatory framework by early 2022[\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-195) in order to position the UK as a global leader in commercializing fusion power.[\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-196) This call was met by the UK government publishing in October 2021 both its _Fusion Green Paper_ and its _Fusion Strategy_, to regulate and commercialize fusion, respectively.[\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-197)[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-198)[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-199) Then, in April 2023, in a decision likely to influence other nuclear regulators, the NRC announced in a unanimous vote that fusion energy would be regulated not as fission but under the same regulatory regime as particle accelerators.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-200)

Then, in October 2023 the UK government, in enacting the [Energy Act 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_Act_2023 "Energy Act 2023"), made the UK the first country to legislate for fusion separately from fission, to support planning and investment, including the UK's planned prototype fusion power plant for 2040; [STEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spherical_Tokamak_for_Energy_Production "Spherical Tokamak for Energy Production")[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-201) the UK is working with Canada and Japan in this regard.[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-202) Meanwhile, in February 2024 the US House of Representatives passed the Atomic Energy Advancement Act, which includes the Fusion Energy Act, which establishes a regulatory framework for fusion energy systems.[\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-203)

## Geopolitics

Given the potential of fusion to transform the world's [energy industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_industry "Energy industry") and mitigate [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change"),[\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-regulation-204)[\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-205) fusion science has traditionally been seen as an integral part of peace-building [science diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_diplomacy "Science diplomacy").[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-quest-206)[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-ITER-135) However, technological developments[\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-207) and private sector involvement has raised concerns over intellectual property, regulatory administration, global leadership;[\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-regulation-204) equity, and potential weaponization.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Diffusion-139)[\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Urgent_Action-208) These challenge ITER's peace-building role and led to calls for a global commission.[\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Urgent_Action-208)[\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-209) Fusion power significantly contributing to climate change by 2050 seems unlikely without substantial breakthroughs and a space race mentality emerging,[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Tokamak-169)[\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-210) but a contribution by 2100 appears possible, with the extent depending on the type and particularly cost of technology pathways.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Energy_Mix-141)[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-211)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/1960-_Articles_published_in_journal_Nuclear_Fusion%2C_by_author_nationality.svg/500px-1960-_Articles_published_in_journal_Nuclear_Fusion%2C_by_author_nationality.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1960-_Articles_published_in_journal_Nuclear_Fusion,_by_author_nationality.svg) The US has been counting on private industry to lead in fusion power, while more recently China's government has made fusion a national priority.[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-NYTimes_20251213-212) In 2025, $2.1 billion was poured into a single Chinese state-owned fusion company, an amount two and a half times the U.S. Energy Department's annual fusion budget.[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-NYTimes_20251213-212)

Developments from late 2020 onwards have led to talk of a "new space race" with multiple entrants, pitting the US against China[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-5big-46) and the UK's [STEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spherical_Tokamak_for_Energy_Production "Spherical Tokamak for Energy Production") FPP,[\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-213)[\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-214) with China now outspending the US and threatening to leapfrog US technology.[\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-215)[\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-216) On September 24, 2020, the United States House of Representatives approved a research and commercialization program. The Fusion Energy Research section incorporated a milestone-based, cost-sharing, [public-private partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership "Public-private partnership") program modeled on [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA")'s COTS program, which launched the commercial [space industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_industry "Space industry").[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Space_Race-157) In February 2021, the National Academies published _Bringing Fusion to the U.S. Grid_, recommending a market-driven, cost-sharing plant for 2035–2040,[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-217)[\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-218)[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-219) and the launch of the Congressional Bipartisan Fusion Caucus followed.[\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-220)

In December 2020, an independent expert panel reviewed [EUROfusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EUROfusion "EUROfusion")'s design and R&D work on DEMO, and EUROfusion confirmed it was proceeding with its Roadmap to Fusion Energy, beginning the conceptual design of DEMO in partnership with the European fusion community, suggesting an EU-backed machine had entered the race.[\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-221)

In October 2023, the UK-oriented Agile Nations group announced a fusion working group.[\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-222) One month later, the UK and the US announced a bilateral partnership to accelerate fusion energy. Then, in December 2023 at [COP28](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COP28 "COP28") the US announced a US global strategy to commercialize fusion energy.[\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-223) Then, in April 2024, Japan and the US announced a similar partnership,[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-224) and in May of the same year, the [G7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G7 "G7") announced a G7 Working Group on Fusion Energy to promote international collaborations to accelerate the development of commercial energy and promote R&D between countries, as well as rationalize fusion regulation.[\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-225) Later the same year, the US partnered with the IAEA to launch the [Fusion Energy Solutions Taskforce](https://usionenergysolutionstaskforce.org/), to collaboratively crowdsource ideas to accelerate commercial fusion energy, in line with the US COP28 statement.

Specifically to resolve the tritium supply problem, in February 2024, the UK ( [UKAEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UKAEA "UKAEA")) and Canada ( [Canadian Nuclear Laboratories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Nuclear_Laboratories_Research_Facilities "Canadian Nuclear Laboratories Research Facilities")) announced an agreement by which Canada could refurbish its [Candu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CANDU_reactor "CANDU reactor") deuterium-uranium tritium-generating heavywater nuclear plants and even build new ones, guaranteeing a supply of tritium into the 2070s, while the UKAEA would test breeder materials and simulate how tritium could be captured, purified, and injected back into the fusion reaction.[\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-226)

In 2024, both [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korea "South Korea") and [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan "Japan") announced major initiatives to accelerate their national fusion strategies, by building electricity-generating public-private fusion plants in the 2030s, aiming to begin operations in the 2040s and 2030s respectively.[\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-227)[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-228)

## Advantages

Fusion power promises to provide more energy for a given weight of fuel than any fuel-consuming energy source currently in use.[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-229) The fuel (primarily [deuterium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deuterium "Deuterium")) exists abundantly in the ocean: about 1 in 6500 hydrogen atoms in seawater is deuterium.[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-230) Although this is only about 0.015%, seawater is plentiful and easy to access, implying that fusion could supply the world's energy needs for millions of years.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Ongena-144)[\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-231)

First generation fusion plants are expected to use the deuterium-tritium fuel cycle. This will require the use of lithium for breeding of the tritium. It is not known for how long global lithium supplies will suffice to supply this need as well as those of the battery and metallurgical industries. It is expected that second generation plants will move on to the more formidable deuterium-deuterium reaction. The deuterium-helium-3 reaction is also of interest, but the light helium isotope is practically non-existent on Earth. It is thought to exist in useful quantities in the [lunar regolith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_regolith "Lunar regolith"), and is abundant in the atmospheres of the gas giant planets.

Fusion power could be used for so-called "deep space" propulsion within the [Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_System "Solar System")[\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-232)[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-233) and for [interstellar space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_space "Interstellar space") exploration where [solar energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_energy "Solar energy") is not available, including via [antimatter-fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antimatter-catalyzed_nuclear_pulse_propulsion "Antimatter-catalyzed nuclear pulse propulsion") hybrid drives.[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-234)[\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-235)

### Helium production

See also: [Helium production in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helium_production_in_the_United_States "Helium production in the United States")

[Deuterium–tritium fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deuterium%E2%80%93tritium_fusion "Deuterium–tritium fusion") produces [helium-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helium-4 "Helium-4") as a [by-product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/By-product "By-product").[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-236)

## Disadvantages

Fusion power has a number of disadvantages. Because 80 percent of the energy in any reactor fueled by deuterium and tritium appears in the form of neutron streams, such reactors share many of the drawbacks of fission reactors. This includes the production of large quantities of [radioactive waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#Radioactive_waste) and serious radiation damage to reactor components. Additionally, naturally occurring tritium is extremely rare. While the hope is that fusion reactors can breed their own tritium, tritium self-sufficiency is extremely challenging, not least because tritium is difficult to contain (tritium has leaked from 48 of 65 nuclear sites in the US[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-237)). In any case the reserve and start-up tritium inventory requirements are likely to be unacceptably large.[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-TritiumSelfSufficiency_Abdou-238)

If reactors can be made to operate using only deuterium fuel, then the tritium replenishment issue is eliminated and neutron radiation damage may be reduced. However, the probabilities of deuterium-deuterium reactions are about 20 times lower than for deuterium-tritium. Additionally, the temperature needed is about 3 times higher than for deuterium-tritium (see [cross section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#Cross_section)). The higher temperatures and lower reaction rates thus significantly complicate the engineering challenges.

### Early experiments

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9b/Kink_instability_at_Aldermaston.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kink_instability_at_Aldermaston.jpg) Early photo of plasma inside a pinch machine (Imperial College 1950–1951)[![The UK claimed that it had gotten fusion first in 1957 on ZETA, but this claim had to later be withdrawn.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ab/A_sun_of_our_own_newspaper_headline.jpg/500px-A_sun_of_our_own_newspaper_headline.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:A_sun_of_our_own_newspaper_headline.jpg) The UK claimed that it had gotten fusion first in 1957 on ZETA, but this claim had to later be withdrawn.

The first machine to achieve controlled [thermonuclear fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermonuclear_fusion "Thermonuclear fusion") was a [pinch machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinch_(plasma_physics) "Pinch (plasma physics)") at Los Alamos National Laboratory called Scylla I at the start of 1958. The team that achieved it was led by a British scientist named [James Tuck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_L._Tuck "James L. Tuck") and included a young [Marshall Rosenbluth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Rosenbluth "Marshall Rosenbluth"). Tuck had been involved in the Manhattan project, but had switched to working on fusion in the early 1950s. He applied for funding for the project as part of a White House sponsored contest to develop a fusion reactor along with [Lyman Spitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyman_Spitzer "Lyman Spitzer"). The previous year, 1957, the British had claimed that they had achieved thermonuclear fusion reactions on the [Zeta pinch machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZETA_(fusion_reactor) "ZETA (fusion reactor)"). However, it turned out that the neutrons they had detected were from beam-target interactions, not fusion, and they withdrew the claim. A [CERN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CERN "CERN")-sponsored study group on controlled thermonuclear fusion met from 1958 to 1964. This group ceased when it became clear that CERN discontinued its limited support for plasma physics.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-239)

Scylla I was a classified machine at the time, so the achievement was hidden from the public. A traditional [Z-pinch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z-pinch "Z-pinch") passes a current down the center of a plasma, which makes a magnetic force around the outside which squeezes the plasma to fusion conditions. Scylla I was a [θ-pinch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theta_pinch "Theta pinch"), which used deuterium to pass a current around the outside of its cylinder to create a magnetic force in the center.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Seife,_Charles_2008-36)[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Phillips,_James_2013-37) After the success of Scylla I, Los Alamos went on to build multiple pinch machines over the next few years.

Spitzer continued his stellarator research at Princeton. While fusion did not immediately transpire, the effort led to the creation of the [Princeton Plasma Physics Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_Plasma_Physics_Laboratory "Princeton Plasma Physics Laboratory").[\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-240)[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-241)

### First tokamak

In the early 1950s, Soviet physicists [I.E. Tamm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Tamm "Igor Tamm") and [A.D. Sakharov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.D._Sakharov "A.D. Sakharov") developed the concept of the tokamak, combining a low-power pinch device with a low-power stellarator.[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-quest-206) [A.D. Sakharov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.D._Sakharov "A.D. Sakharov")'s group constructed the first tokamaks, achieving the first quasistationary fusion reaction.[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-242):90 The Soviet Union built a series of tokamaks starting in 1955. The [T-3 tokamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-3_tokamak "T-3 tokamak") started operating in the early 1960s, and its record-breaking performance was presented at a world-wide conference in 1968.[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-iter-iaea68-243)

Over time, the "advanced tokamak" concept emerged, which included non-circular plasma, internal diverters and limiters, superconducting magnets, operation in the "H-mode" island of increased stability,[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-244) and the compact tokamak, with the magnets on the inside of the vacuum chamber.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-245)[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-246)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/TMX_Baseball_Coils.jpg/500px-TMX_Baseball_Coils.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TMX_Baseball_Coils.jpg) Magnetic mirrors suffered from end losses, requiring high power, complex magnetic designs, such as the baseball coil pictured here.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Novette_laser.jpg/330px-Novette_laser.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Novette_laser.jpg)

The Novette target chamber (metal sphere with diagnostic devices protruding radially), which was reused from the [Shiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiva_laser "Shiva laser") project and two newly built laser chains visible in background

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Fusion_target_implosion_on_NOVA_laser.jpg/330px-Fusion_target_implosion_on_NOVA_laser.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fusion_target_implosion_on_NOVA_laser.jpg)

Inertial confinement fusion implosion on the [Nova laser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_laser "Nova laser") during the 1980s was a key driver of fusion development.

### First inertial confinement experiments

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Shiva_amplifier_chains.jpg/500px-Shiva_amplifier_chains.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Shiva_amplifier_chains.jpg)

Shiva laser, 1977, the largest ICF laser system built in the seventies

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/The_Tandem_Mirror_Experiment.jpg/500px-The_Tandem_Mirror_Experiment.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_Tandem_Mirror_Experiment.jpg)

The Tandem Mirror Experiment (TMX) in 1979

Laser fusion was suggested in 1962 by scientists at [Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Livermore_National_Laboratory "Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory") (LLNL), shortly after the invention of the laser in 1960. [Inertial confinement fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertial_confinement_fusion "Inertial confinement fusion") experiments using lasers began as early as 1965.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] Several laser systems were built at LLNL, including the [Argus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argus_laser "Argus laser"), the [Cyclops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclops_laser "Cyclops laser"), the [Janus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janus_laser "Janus laser"), the [long path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_path_laser "Long path laser"), the [Shiva laser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiva_laser "Shiva laser"), and the [Nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_(laser) "Nova (laser)").[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-247)

Laser advances included frequency-tripling crystals that transformed infrared laser beams into ultraviolet beams and "chirping", which changed a single wavelength into a full spectrum that could be amplified and then reconstituted into one frequency.[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-248) Laser research cost over one billion dollars in the 1980s.[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-NRDC-249)

### 1980s

The [PLT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_Large_Torus "Princeton Large Torus"), [TFTR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TFTR "TFTR"), [Tore Supra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tore_Supra "Tore Supra"), [JET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_European_Torus "Joint European Torus"), [T-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-15_(reactor) "T-15 (reactor)"), and [JT-60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JT-60 "JT-60") tokamaks were built and operated in the 1980s.
[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-250)[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-251)[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-252) In 1984, Martin Peng of ORNL proposed the [spherical tokamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spherical_tokamak "Spherical tokamak") with a much smaller radius.[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-253) It used a single large conductor in the center, with magnets as half-rings off this conductor. The aspect ratio fell to as low as 1.2.[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Small_Tight-254):B247[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-255):225 Peng's advocacy caught the interest of [Derek Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derek_Robinson_(physicist) "Derek Robinson (physicist)"), who built the [Small Tight Aspect Ratio Tokamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Tight_Aspect_Ratio_Tokamak "Small Tight Aspect Ratio Tokamak"), (START).[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Small_Tight-254)

### 1990s

In 1991, the Preliminary Tritium Experiment at the [Joint European Torus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_European_Torus "Joint European Torus") achieved the world's first controlled release of fusion power.[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-256)

In 1993, TFTR became the first tokamak to conduct experiments with significant mixes of deuterium and tritium. In 1994 these experiments resulted in a discharge with the world record 10.1 MW fusion power with 39.9 MW of neutral beam heating power. The ratio Q is 0.26. The ratio in the plasma core, Q was approximately 0.8.[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-257)

In 1996, Tore Supra created a plasma for two minutes with a current of almost 1million amperes, totaling 280 MJ of injected and extracted energy.[\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-258)

In 1997, JET produced a peak of 16.1MW of fusion power (65% of heat to plasma[\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-259)), with fusion power of over 10MW sustained for over 0.5sec.[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-260)

### 2000s

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b2/MAST_plasma_image.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MAST_plasma_image.jpg) The [Mega Ampere Spherical Tokamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mega_Ampere_Spherical_Tokamak "Mega Ampere Spherical Tokamak") became operational in the UK in 1999.

"Fast ignition"[\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-261)[\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-262) saved power and moved ICF into the race for energy production.

In 2006, China's [Experimental Advanced Superconducting Tokamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_Advanced_Superconducting_Tokamak "Experimental Advanced Superconducting Tokamak") (EAST) test reactor was completed.[\[263\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-263) It was the first tokamak to use superconducting magnets to generate both toroidal and poloidal fields.

In March 2009, the laser-driven ICF [NIF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Ignition_Facility "National Ignition Facility") became operational.[\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-264)

In the 2000s, privately backed fusion companies entered the race, including [TAE Technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TAE_Technologies "TAE Technologies"),[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-265) [General Fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Fusion "General Fusion"),[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-266)[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-267) and [Tokamak Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokamak_Energy "Tokamak Energy").[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-268)

### 2010s

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Preamplifier_at_the_National_Ignition_Facility.jpg/500px-Preamplifier_at_the_National_Ignition_Facility.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Preamplifier_at_the_National_Ignition_Facility.jpg)

The preamplifiers of the National Ignition Facility (NIF). In 2012, it achieved a 500-terawatt shot.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Wendelstein7-X_Torushall-2011.jpg/500px-Wendelstein7-X_Torushall-2011.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wendelstein7-X_Torushall-2011.jpg)

The Wendelstein7X [stellarator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellarator "Stellarator") under construction.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/W7X-Spulen_Plasma_blau_gelb.jpg/500px-W7X-Spulen_Plasma_blau_gelb.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:W7X-Spulen_Plasma_blau_gelb.jpg)

Example of a stellarator design: A coil system (blue) surrounds plasma (yellow), with a magnetic field line (green) on the yellow plasma surface.

Private and public research accelerated in the 2010s. General Fusion developed plasma injector technology and Tri Alpha Energy tested its C-2U device.[\[269\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-269) The French [Laser Mégajoule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laser_M%C3%A9gajoule "Laser Mégajoule") began operation. NIF achieved net energy gain[\[270\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-270) in 2013, as defined in the very limited sense as the hot spot at the core of the collapsed target, rather than the whole target.[\[271\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-271)

In 2014, [Phoenix Nuclear Labs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Nuclear_Labs "Phoenix Nuclear Labs") sold a high-yield [neutron generator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_generator "Neutron generator") that could sustain 5×1011 [deuterium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deuterium "Deuterium") fusion reactions per second over a 24-hour period.[\[272\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-272)

In 2015, [MIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIT "MIT") announced a [tokamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokamak "Tokamak") it named the [ARC fusion reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ARC_fusion_reactor "ARC fusion reactor"), using [rare-earth barium-copper oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare-earth_barium-copper_oxide "Rare-earth barium-copper oxide") (REBCO) superconducting tapes to produce high-magnetic field coils that it claimed could produce comparable magnetic field strength in a smaller configuration than other designs.[\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-273)

In October, researchers at the [Max Planck Institute of Plasma Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Planck_Institute_of_Plasma_Physics "Max Planck Institute of Plasma Physics") in Greifswald, Germany, completed building the largest [stellarator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellarator "Stellarator") to date, the [Wendelstein 7-X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wendelstein_7-X "Wendelstein 7-X") (W7-X). The W7-X stellarator began Operational phase 1 (OP1.1) on December 10, 2015, successfully producing helium plasma.[\[274\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-274) The objective was to test vital systems and understand the machine's physics. By February 2016, hydrogen plasma was achieved, with temperatures reaching up to 100 million Kelvin. The initial tests used five graphite limiters. After over 2,000 pulses and achieving significant milestones, OP1.1 concluded on March 10, 2016. An upgrade followed, and OP1.2 in 2017 aimed to test an uncooled divertor. By June 2018, record temperatures were reached. W7-X concluded its first campaigns with limiter and island divertor tests, achieving notable advancements by the end of 2018.[\[275\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-275)[\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-276)[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-277) It soon produced helium and hydrogen plasmas lasting up to 30 minutes.[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-278)

The UK's Tokamak Energy's [ST40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokamak_Energy "Tokamak Energy") generated "first plasma".[\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-279) The next year, [Eni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eni "Eni") announced a $50million investment in [Commonwealth Fusion Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Fusion_Systems "Commonwealth Fusion Systems"), to attempt to commercialize MIT's [ARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ARC_fusion_reactor "ARC fusion reactor") technology.[\[280\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-280)[\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-281)[\[282\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-282)[\[283\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-283)

### 2020s

In January 2021, SuperOx announced the commercialization of a new [superconducting wire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superconducting_wire "Superconducting wire") with more than 700 A/mm2 current capability.[\[284\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-284)

TAE Technologies announced results for its Norman device, holding a temperature of about 60MK for 30 milliseconds, 8 and 10 times higher, respectively, than the company's previous devices.[\[285\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-285)

In October, Oxford-based [First Light Fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Light_Fusion "First Light Fusion") revealed its projectile fusion project, which fires an aluminum disc at a fusion target, accelerated by a 9 mega-amp electrical pulse, reaching speeds of 20 kilometres per second (12mi/s). The resulting fusion generates neutrons whose energy is captured as heat.[\[286\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-286)

On November 8, in an invited talk to the 63rd Annual Meeting of the APS Division of Plasma Physics,[\[287\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-287) the National Ignition Facility claimed[\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-physics_v14_168-288) to have triggered [fusion ignition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_ignition "Fusion ignition") in the laboratory on August 8, 2021, for the first time in the 60+ year history of the ICF program.[\[289\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-289)[\[290\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-290) The shot yielded 1.3 MJ of fusion energy, an over 8X improvement on tests done in spring of 2021.[\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-physics_v14_168-288) NIF estimates that 230kJ of energy reached the fuel capsule, which resulted in an almost 6-fold energy output from the capsule.[\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-physics_v14_168-288) A researcher from Imperial College London stated that the majority of the field agreed that ignition had been demonstrated.[\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-physics_v14_168-288)

In November 2021, [Helion Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helion_Energy "Helion Energy") reported receiving $500 million in Series E funding for its seventh-generation Polaris device, designed to demonstrate net electricity production, with an additional $1.7billion of commitments tied to specific milestones,[\[291\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-291) while Commonwealth Fusion Systems raised an additional $1.8billion in Series B funding to construct and operate its [SPARC tokamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SPARC_(tokamak) "SPARC (tokamak)"), the single largest investment in any private fusion company.[\[292\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-292)

In April 2022, First Light announced that their hypersonic projectile fusion prototype had produced neutrons compatible with fusion. Their technique electromagnetically fires projectiles at [Mach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mach "Mach") 19 at a caged fuel pellet. The deuterium fuel is compressed at Mach 204, reaching pressure levels of 100 TPa.[\[293\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-293)

On December 13, 2022, the [US Department of Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Department_of_Energy "US Department of Energy") reported that researchers at the National Ignition Facility had achieved a net energy gain from a fusion reaction. The reaction of hydrogen fuel at the facility produced about 3.15 MJ of energy while consuming 2.05 MJ of input. However, while the fusion reactions may have produced more than 3 megajoules of energy—more than was delivered to the target—NIF's 192 lasers consumed 322 MJ of grid energy in the conversion process.[\[294\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-NYT-209221213-294)[\[295\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-Ignition-295)[\[296\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-WP-20221212-296)[\[297\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-297)

In May 2023, the [United States Department of Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Energy "United States Department of Energy") (DOE) provided a grant of $46 million to eight companies across seven states to support fusion power plant design and research efforts. This funding, under the Milestone-Based Fusion Development Program, aligns with objectives to demonstrate pilot-scale fusion within a decade and to develop fusion as a carbon-neutral energy source by 2050. The granted companies are tasked with addressing the scientific and technical challenges to create viable fusion pilot plant designs in the next 5–10 years. The recipient firms include [Commonwealth Fusion Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Fusion_Systems "Commonwealth Fusion Systems"), Focused Energy Inc., Princeton Stellarators Inc., Realta Fusion Inc., Tokamak Energy Inc., Type One Energy Group, Xcimer Energy Inc., and Zap Energy Inc.[\[298\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-298)

In December 2023, the largest and most advanced tokamak JT-60SA was inaugurated in [Naka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naka,_Ibaraki "Naka, Ibaraki"), Japan. The reactor is a joint project between Japan and the European Union. The reactor had achieved its first plasma in October 2023.[\[299\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-299) Subsequently, South Korea's fusion reactor project, the [Korean Superconducting Tokamak Advanced Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Superconducting_Tokamak_Advanced_Research "Korean Superconducting Tokamak Advanced Research"), successfully operated for 102 seconds in a high-containment mode (H-mode) containing high ion temperatures of more than 100 million degrees in plasma tests conducted from December 2023 to February 2024.[\[300\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-300)

In January 2025, EAST fusion reactor in China was reported to maintain a steady-state high-confinement plasma operation for 1066 seconds (nearly 18 minutes).[\[301\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-301) In February 2025, the French Alternative Energies and Atomic Energy Commission (CEA) announced that its [WEST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WEST_(formerly_Tore_Supra) "WEST (formerly Tore Supra)") tokamak had maintained a stable plasma for 1,337 seconds—over 22 minutes.[\[302\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-302) In March Energy Singularity announced that a magnet that produce a magnetic field of 21.7 [T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesla_(unit) "Tesla (unit)").[\[303\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-303)

In 2026, EAST announced that it had surpassed the Greenwald plasma density limit. The experiment pioneered plasma-wall self-organization, electron cyclotron resonance heating (ohmic start-up), management of initial fuel gas pressure, active reduction of boundary impurity sputtering and contamination from the reactor walls. The plasma entered a density-free regime where higher density did not increase instability.[\[304\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-304)

## Future development

Claims of commercially viable fusion power being relatively imminent have often attracted ridicule within the scientific community.[\[305\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-305) A common joke is that human-engineered fusion has always been promised as 30 years away since the concept was first discussed,[\[306\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-306) or that it has been "20 years away for 50 years".[\[307\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-307)

In 2024, [Commonwealth Fusion Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Fusion_Systems "Commonwealth Fusion Systems") announced plans to build the world's first grid-scale commercial nuclear fusion power plant at the James River Industrial Center in [Chesterfield County, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chesterfield_County,_Virginia "Chesterfield County, Virginia"), which is part of the [Greater Richmond Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Richmond_Region "Greater Richmond Region"). The plant is designed to produce about 400 MW of electric power, and is intended to come online in the early 2030s.[\[308\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-308)[\[309\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-309)[\[310\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-310)

In 2025, [Helion Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helion_Energy "Helion Energy"), a startup company backed by [OpenAI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenAI "OpenAI"), announced plans to build a fusion power plant in [Malaga, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaga,_Washington "Malaga, Washington") to supply power to [Microsoft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft "Microsoft") data centers by 2028. It will use extant grid infrastructure for the nearby [Rock Island Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Island_Dam "Rock Island Dam") hydroelectric plant.[\[311\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-311)

In January 2026, Type One Energy announced plans to build a 350MWe fusion power plant using a stellarator fusion reactor.[\[312\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_power#cite_note-312)
